One exception was approved for now (C-06): pyannote moves from speaker-diarization-3.1 to community-1. Where an approved row leaves an input to the Principal (the D-16 context targets, the D-47 speed floors, the D-46 and D-49 test sets), the stated default is built and a to-do raised. The uncensored-seat items (D-07, D-08, D-09, D-58, N-10, P-05, P-17, S-05) are unchanged by this revision and remain the Principal's to configure. Until v0.4.0 the record read: every decision confirmed, every resolution accepted, every risk acknowledged, every pre-execution item ticked in the workbook returned 2026-09-21; the risks added since (R22 to R25) were acknowledged by the Principal on 2026-10-08; R26 to R28, recorded on 2026-10-12 with the S53 changes, were acknowledged by the Principal on 2026-10-13; in the same returned workbook the Principal confirmed that Secure Boot is enabled in the BIOS and the USB recovery drive is in hand. Nothing has been executed. The Day 1 scripts under `scripts/day1/` are written against this document; the fact-checking that preceded them corrected the baseline in the places listed in Section 23. D15, reopened by the build, was closed by the Principal on 2026-10-05 as option (a): Secure Boot enabled. Policy v0.3.3 (the Principal, 2026-10-05): a missing input from the Principal never stops a phase; the scripts ask once, defer the item and keep a to-do list for the live ATLAS. On 2026-10-08 the Principal re-confirmed Ubuntu Server after a sourced comparison with Fedora 44 KDE Plasma (C27: "stability is key to a good AI node"); the comparison also found five script defects, fixed in v0.3.4 (Section 23, S32 to S38). On 2026-10-09 the Principal asked whether the report "Linux 7.4 to support the NPU on Ryzen AI Max 400 Gorgon Halo" needed adopting: it does not (a device-name patch, 3.7); at the Principal's request v0.3.5 corrects the NPU's reason, adds the platform watch-list, fixes the Arbiter's memory budget, holds the GA kernel line and fixes V3a for the current kernel (S44 to S47). On 2026-10-10 the Principal made two decisions. Option (c) (S48): Day 1 reclaims the crash-kernel memory that Ubuntu's kdump-tools reserves at every boot, switches kdump off without purging it, adds `panic=10` to the kernel line so a kernel panic restarts the node, and returns the Arbiter's host reserve to 16 GiB. And S49: Day 1 adds `kho=off` to the kernel line, switching off the kexec handover that Ubuntu's 7.0 kernel enables by default and that would otherwise keep about 13 GiB of memory where GPU memory can never be placed. On 2026-10-11 the Principal decided S50, "add the hardware watchdog": Day 1 loads the platform's watchdog driver by name at every boot (Ubuntu blacklists every watchdog module) and has systemd arm it with a two-minute timeout, so a node whose kernel or systemd stops is reset by the hardware and unlocks its disks unattended, as after `panic=10`; where this board has no usable hardware watchdog a software one stands in, recorded as deferred with a to-do (3.6, V24). Also on 2026-10-11 the Principal asked whether Ubuntu would adopt AMD's ROCm 10.1 (released 2026-10-05): it does not, by design (3.4). The review found one way a 10.1 build could still reach the Phase 4 image, an unpinned torchaudio; the Principal approved pinning it and correcting the document. This is a fix, not a new decision: Phase 4 stays on ROCm 10.0.0 until a deliberate re-test after Day 1 (S51). Later on 2026-10-11 the Principal asked about an XDA article saying that local LLMs waste memory on context that is never used. ATLAS already sets every context explicitly (4.3), but the review found two gaps, which the Principal approved fixing (S52): the everyday text-and-vision pair would not have formed in production, because the vision engine's two-slot profile was only ever set temporarily, and context shift, on which 4.3 relied so that generation never hard-stops, is switched off on six of the eight chat engines in the pinned llama.cpp. The vision engine's two-slot profile is now its standing one, and the orchestrator keeps every request inside its engine's per-slot window. Phase 3 also measures each engine's real context memory and the host-RAM caches outside the Arbiter's budget, and raises them as two to-dos for the Principal. On 2026-10-12 the Principal asked whether the 72-hour semantic pruning (9.6) should be improved or made usage-adaptive. The review found the design sound for Day 1 and the first months, with three small gaps the Principal approved closing before Day 1 (S53): the window setting was invisible and, once written, would have been reset by any re-run of its step; a sweep that failed inside the worker was silent for three days at a time; and the graph archive carried no document text. Measuring pruning against real use and bringing retention proposals through the approval queue are recorded as phased items with the events that bring each in; fully automatic adjustment is excluded (16.3 item 6) and the D9 values stay closed; no decision changes. |

## How to read this document

Every item carries one of these markers:

| Marker | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **AGREED** | Settled in the brief. Build to this. |
| **RESOLVED** | A contradiction or ambiguity was found during the sweep and is resolved here. Read these; they change earlier text. |
| **CLOSED** | A decision the Principal has answered. The answer is stated; no further input needed. Section 19 lists them all. |
| **VERIFY** | Cannot be confirmed from the desk. Day 1 must prove it. Numbered V1…Vn, listed in Section 21. |
| **RISK** | A known risk with a mitigation. Numbered R1…Rn, listed in Section 20. |

Sections 1–17 are the consolidated framework. Sections 18–22 are the audit. Appendices carry configuration reference and sources.

---

## 0. Executive summary

**Verdict.** The framework is coherent, fully decided, and buildable. All twenty-seven decisions are closed (D15 on 2026-10-05, D16 to D27 on 2026-10-16), all twenty original contradictions accepted, and ten further resolutions have been added since v0.1 (C21 to C26 in v0.2, C27 in v0.3.4, C28 to C30 in v0.4.0). The Day 1 scripts are written against this document and, since v0.3.3, never stop on a missing input from the Principal: they ask once, defer, and keep a to-do list for the live ATLAS. *v0.4.0:* the Principal's approvals of 2026-10-16 reopen D4, D10 and D12 through D16, D17 and D18 and supersede C23, C25 and C26 with C28, C29 and C30 (Sections 18, 19); every new decision is answered, so the framework stays fully decided.

**What changed since v0.1, in order of consequence:**

1. **The machine changed (Section 2).** GMKtec EVO-X5 Pro replaces the MINISFORUM MS-S1 Max: 192 GB of memory instead of 128 GB, 273 GB/s instead of ~256, and both NVMe slots at four lanes instead of one fast and one slow. The GPU reports the same `gfx1151` identifier, confirmed by the Principal, so every piece of community ROCm and Vulkan work carries over unchanged and risks R1 and R3 stand exactly as written (both were amended later for reasons unrelated to the machine: R1 in S15, both in S51).
2. **Quantisation: the least-quantised rung that fits (Section 5.1; rewritten in v0.4.0).** The extra memory buys accuracy, now under the Principal's standing rule (Section 1): each seat runs the least-quantised rung that fits it alone, with its measured quality cost stated. DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 moves from `UD-Q4_K_XL` to `UD-Q8_K_XL`, bit-exact to its released weights (D-11 (a)); GLM-5.3-Flash runs at `UD-IQ4_XS`, the one lossy rung, provisional until the Phase 3 KLD run (T-03, D-02); gpt-oss-120b moves to the F16-trunk GGUF, its experts still in their native MXFP4 (D-59 (b)). The KV caches follow: f16 where the cache is cheap, `q8_0` elsewhere, `q4_0` on no new entry (D-29 (a), 4.3). v0.3.5's `Q8_0` engines, Nemotron 3 Super, Qwen3.5-122B, Qwen2.5-VL-72B and Meditron, leave the Day 1 roster (D-13, D-21); Nemotron returns as a phased pull.
3. **The roster is rebuilt around two 300B-class seats (Section 5.1; rewritten in v0.4.0).** GLM-5.3-Flash (320B MoE, 18B active, `UD-IQ4_XS`, 157.95 GB with its glm5v vision projector) carries Ren, Gideon, Silas, Helena, Minerva, Victor and Alaric, Eleanor's persona work, vision and the long-document route. DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 (284B MoE, 13B active, `UD-Q8_K_XL`, 161.87 GB) carries Arthur and stays the Apex engine for TF_OMEGA. The two form the Deep Think pair (D-22). gpt-oss-120b becomes the fast utility and the fallback floor every seat can use (D-12, D-40), and Qwen3.5-4B stays the always-resident router. GLM needs a newer llama.cpp, so the pin moves from v0.4.1 to build tag b11474 (3.4). The specialist seats (Intern-S1 for science, Ling-3.0-flash-Fin for finance, Baichuan-M3 as the medical cross-check, Qwen3-Coder-Next for code) and the Qwen3.8-Flash-Next and Nemotron 3 Super pulls are phased after Day 1 (D-26 (c), D-42 (b)).
4. **One LLM seat is resident at a time, and only one generates (Section 4.2; rewritten in v0.4.0, D-32 (a)).** The two large Day 1 seats are 147 to 151 GiB of weights, and every seat, gpt-oss-120b included, runs alone with the whole engine budget; the always-resident companions do not count. v0.3.5's two-engine residency and its text-and-vision pair are withdrawn (superseding C26); the limit is a setting, so pairing can return without a code change. Simultaneous generation stays excluded: the memory bandwidth is shared, so two streams would each run at half speed. The Celery GPU queue keeps its single worker. A swap between two large seats now costs about 50 to 85 seconds plus re-prefill (INFERRED, measured in Phase 3 by T-14), which is why GLM, carrying most seats, stays resident between them (D-23).
5. **The node gets a graphical desktop (Section 3.1).** Ubuntu Server 26.04.1 stays the base, with XFCE added on top and xrdp for remote graphical access from the Principal's Windows PC. This is not the full Ubuntu Desktop image; XFCE costs roughly 300 to 500 MB idle against GNOME's 1 to 1.5 GB, and the AI system itself remains browser-served and independent of any desktop session.
6. **Six domains added, bringing the roster to thirty-six (Section 8.2).** Five further areas the Principal named are folded into existing domains as explicit subspecialties rather than duplicated as new cards.
7. **Vision moves onto GLM-5.3-Flash (Section 15.2; rewritten in v0.4.0, D-20 (a)).** v0.3.5's sole vision engine, Qwen2.5-VL-72B, is retired (D-21). The glm5v projector (1.13 GB) loads on the GLM unit itself, so a switch from text to vision costs no swap while GLM is resident, and v0.3.5's 2-slot co-resident vision profile is gone (S-22). A small OCR companion for scanned documents (C-04) is phased after Day 1.
8. **Apple `.ipa` builds removed entirely (Section 15.1).** The Principal owns no Mac and rules out a cloud runner. Android and Windows builds stay; iOS source is still written, but compiled elsewhere whenever Mac access exists.
9. **Ubuntu 24.04.5 evaluated and rejected (Section 3.1); Fedora 44 KDE Plasma evaluated and rejected (C27, v0.3.4).** 24.04.5 shares the 7.0 kernel through HWE but stays on Mesa 25.2; 26.04.1 wins on two further years of support (May 2031), a newer graphics stack and toolchain, and its one disadvantage, host ROCm, does not apply to a containerised design (and is gone since AMD's ROCm 10.0.0 supports this chip on 26.04 too, S51). Fedora 44 would need a major-version upgrade about once a year and its KDE edition cannot offer a fresh Remote Desktop login without autologin once Plasma 6.8 ships; Ubuntu's fixed 7.0 kernel series is the stability the node needs.
10. **amd64v3 and amd64v4 archives rejected (Section 3.1).** The gain lands on CPU-bound distribution packages, not GPU inference, and llama.cpp and the PyTorch containers already compile against this exact chip. Canonical keeps the standard baseline as 26.04's default for good reason.

## 1. Mission and principles

**AGREED — Identity.** A.T.L.A.S.: a zero-cloud, autonomous AI node on bare metal, serving one user, the Principal, through a single interface, with two personas (Ren Ackerman, Arthur Sterling), an eight-director Shadow Cabinet, thirty-six domain knowledge profiles, twenty-three cross-domain task forces, and a set of autonomous protocols.

**AGREED — Zero-cloud, precisely defined.** After the Sentinel discussion the rule is:

- No model inference runs anywhere but this node.
- No Principal data leaves the node except to services the Principal already uses and explicitly connects (Google Workspace, Xero, Cloudflare, Wix, Pay.com, RewardPay), through their own authenticated APIs.
- Outbound reads from public data sources are permitted only from a firewall-enforced allowlist (Sentinel feeds, package repositories during build).
- Dynamic DNS name updates to Cloudflare are permitted; they carry no Principal data.
- No hosted AI, no cloud fallback, no telemetry from any installed component.

**AGREED — Single user.** The Principal. All personas address the Principal; the Principal never addresses the Shadow Cabinet directly.

**AGREED — Locale.** Australia. English only at launch; multilingual capability retained for later (Qwen3.5 and Kokoro carry it; *v0.4.0, S-06:* the Qwen3.5 still on the node is the 4B router, beside the multilingual Qwen3 retrieval pair (5.3), since Qwen3.5-122B-A10B, the large multilingual engine of v0.3.5, leaves the Day 1 roster (D-21); how well the Day 1 seats work in another language is untested, UNVERIFIED). Date format, tax treatment in Silas's rules, and Sentinel's market indices are Australian.

**AGREED — Completion standard.** ATLAS never delegates work back to the Principal. A form arrives filled, a document arrives drafted, a booking arrives confirmed. The only things asked of the Principal are decisions and approvals.

**AGREED — Standing rules for the engine arsenal (v0.4.0, S-13: the Principal's directives of 2026-10-15, approved by the Principal on 2026-10-16 in the returned action register).**

- **No LLM seat under about 70B total parameters.** The companions are exempt: the router, the embedder and reranker, and the OCR, speech, diarisation, voice and image engines.
- **Every LLM engine runs alone, with the whole engine budget** (4.1, 4.2 rule 3). The directive's ~164 GiB was the v0.3.5 budget; with the retrieval pair on the CPU it is about 156 GiB expected (4.1).
- **Result quality first, speed second.** No seat is failed for speed against a band nobody has measured on this chip: Phase 3 records each seat's decode beside its projection and fails it only below a floor the Principal sets (V10, D-33 (c)). None is set yet; the default is record only, with the to-do `decode-floors` (D-47).
- **The least-quantised rung that fits, with its measured quality cost stated** (5.1; the Phase 3 quality record and the KLD run T-03, to-do `kld-run`).
- **Uncensored engines stay under R13**, as in v0.3.5.

**Admission of a new engine (v0.4.0, S-13).** v0.3.5 asked for a GGUF that loads on v0.4.1 Vulkan, a footprint inside the 164 GiB budget with the default slot sized for a co-resident partner, and a defined persona or tier role. Now an engine is admitted when it loads on the pinned llama.cpp (3.4) on Vulkan; its exact-byte weights, with the KV cache, per-slot state and compute buffer of its stated profile, fit the engine budget alone (4.1); it meets the 70B floor or is a companion; it has a defined persona, task-force or tier role; and its licence is read and accepted. It is appended to `engines.json` after the Day 1 set, so no Day 1 port moves, and comes in only through a Phase 3 load test.

## 2. Hardware baseline

**AGREED — Machine.** GMKtec EVO-X5 Pro, "Gorgon Halo" platform. This replaces the MINISFORUM MS-S1 Max named in v0.1.

| Component | Specification | Design consequence |
|---|---|---|
| CPU | AMD Ryzen AI Max+ PRO 495, 16 Zen 5 cores, 32 threads, up to 5.2 GHz, 64 MB cache | Ample for orchestrator, Celery CPU workers, vector DB, browser automation, CPU-only tools (Radiance, EnergyPlus, KiCad, Docling) and, since v0.4.0, the Qwen3 4B embedder and reranker (D-14 (e), 4.1) |
| GPU | Radeon 8065S, 40 CUs, RDNA 3.5, **ROCm target gfx1151, confirmed by the Principal** | Runs all inference except the CPU retrieval pair (v0.4.0, 4.1). Same identifier as the previous machine, so every community ROCm, Vulkan and llama.cpp finding carries over unchanged |
| NPU | XDNA 2, up to 55 TOPS | Not used. Zero design dependency (re-checked 2026-10-09, watch-list W-NPU in 3.7). The 26.04 kernel already ships the NPU driver (`amdxdna`) and its firmware, so the NPU may show up as `/dev/accel/accel0`; that is harmless, no container is given it, and step 5 records what the node shows. Linux NPU software now exists (FastFlowLM, maintained by AMD, with an Ubuntu 26.04 package), so v0.3.4's "Windows-first" no longer holds, but it does not fit ATLAS today (3.7) |
| Memory | 192 GB LPDDR5X-8533, soldered | 273 GB/s. Decode speed = bandwidth ÷ active weight bytes. Not upgradeable |
| GPU-addressable memory | All of MemTotal on Linux via GTT once Day 1 has switched off kexec handover (`kho=off`, S49; with it on, Ubuntu's 7.0 kernel keeps about 13 GiB of MemTotal where GPU pages cannot go), about 187 to 188 GiB expected once Day 1 has removed the 4.25 GiB that kdump-tools reserves for a crash kernel (S48), of which **about 156 GiB is free for engines** once the GPU resident set (the router), the CPU retrieval pair and the 16 GiB host reserve are subtracted (4.1; v0.3.5 said about 164 GiB, with the bge pair on the iGPU; v0.3.4 said ~180 GB and ~170 GB) | **VERIFY V3.** The reason to buy this machine, and what makes the two 147 to 151 GiB Day 1 seats possible |
| Power | 45–120 W configurable TDP | Principal is adding external cooling (R10) |
| Expansion | USB4 ports with external GPU dock support; a third M.2 slot unused | A CUDA-locked tool (NVIDIA Modulus/PhysicsNeMo) would need an NVIDIA card via the dock |
| Network | **Wi-Fi only by Principal's choice** | Wired 10GbE not used. V1 is informational, not a gate. Affects Phase 3 and 4 download time, not the VPN or the port forward |
| Storage 1 | 8 TB NVMe, PCIe 4.0 x4 | `/srv/atlas`: models, engines, agent data, vector stores, vault. The growing side |
| Storage 2 | 4 TB NVMe, PCIe 4.0 x4 | Operating system, logs, cold storage archives, restic backups. Both drives are now the same speed, so capacity decides the split, not bandwidth |

**AGREED — Expected inference performance (community benchmarks on this chip family; amended in v0.4.0, S-25).**

| Model class | In memory | Decode |
|---|---|---|
| 8B dense, 4-bit | 5 GB | 40–50 tok/s |
| 30B MoE, 3B active | 18 GB | 70–100 tok/s |
| 32B dense, 4-bit | 19 GB | 10–12 tok/s |
| 70B dense, 4-bit | 40 GB | ~5 tok/s |
| 120B MoE, 5.1B active (gpt-oss-120b) | 63 GB at MXFP4; 65.37 GB as the F16-trunk GGUF of Day 1 (D-59 (b)) | 53.8–55.6 tok/s measured at MXFP4 on 128 GB Ryzen AI Max+ 395 machines (Vulkan); 57–60 projected for the Day 1 file on the PRO 495 (INFERRED) |
| 120B MoE, 10–12.7B active at 4-bit (Nemotron, Qwen3.5-122B) | 65–70 GB | 18–19 tok/s |
| Same two at `Q8_0` (v0.3.5's setting; both leave the Day 1 roster, D-13, D-21) | 120–130 GB | 12–17 tok/s, roughly a quarter slower for near-lossless weights |
| 284B MoE, 13B active (DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731) | 155.10 GB at `UD-Q4_K_XL`; 161.87 GB at `UD-Q8_K_XL`, the Day 1 file (D-11 (a)) | About 7 tok/s projected at `UD-Q8_K_XL` and about 11 at `UD-Q4_K_XL` on mainline Vulkan (INFERRED, a floor: mainline reaches only 43 to 45% of the memory bandwidth on this architecture, tuned forks about 66%). Measured only at 2 to 3 bits on 128 GB Ryzen AI Max+ 395 machines: `UD-IQ2_XXS` 12.6–13.3 on stock Vulkan, `UD-IQ3_XXS` 11.9 on mainline b10488. v0.3.5's 25–32 tok/s had no measurement behind it and is withdrawn |
| 320B MoE, 18B active (GLM-5.3-Flash) | 156.82 GB at `UD-IQ4_XS`, plus 1.13 GB for the glm5v projector: the Day 1 files | 12–14 tok/s projected at `UD-IQ4_XS` (INFERRED). Measured on a PRO 495, the node's chip, for a 3.84 bpw file (143.54 GiB) on Vulkan: 17.4 tok/s at depth 0 and 15.0 at 32k (VERIFIED from its published card); about 6 by 128k in review (UNVERIFIED) |

Prompt processing: ~350 tok/s stock, ~1,000 tok/s tuned, measured on a 7B model; larger models scale down proportionally (v0.4.0: the 3.84 bpw GLM-5.3-Flash file of the table above prefilled 256.4 tok/s at 2,048 tokens and 95.9 at 32k on the PRO 495, VERIFIED from its card). **This number drives the prompt-cache and injection-size rules in Sections 4.4 and 8.4**, and since v0.4.0 the re-prefill cost of every swap (4.2 rule 4).

*v0.4.0 (S-25; D-33 option (c), approved by the Principal on 2026-10-16 in the returned action register):* the 72B dense row left with Qwen2.5-VL-72B (D-21), DeepSeek's unsupported 25–32 tok/s is withdrawn, and the Day 1 seats have their own rows. Every figure above is another machine's measurement or a projection. Phase 3 records each seat's measured decode beside its projection and fails a seat only below a per-seat floor the Principal sets (V10); a seat on an architecture newer than the old pin (GLM-5.3-Flash) also gets a deferrable row like the Apex engine's (V22). No floor is set yet: the default is record only, with the to-do `decode-floors` (D-47).

## 3. Platform and operating system

### 3.1 Operating system — AGREED with one refinement

- **Ubuntu Server 26.04.1 LTS**, with **XFCE** installed on top and **xrdp** for remote graphical access. **RESOLVED (C19, reopened and re-decided):** v0.1 specified Server with no graphical layer; the Principal requires a graphics interface. The answer is not the full Ubuntu Desktop image, which ships GNOME and costs 1 to 1.5 GB idle, but Server plus XFCE at roughly 300 to 500 MB. The Principal opens a real desktop session, with a real browser, from the Remote Desktop client already built into Windows. The AI system itself, Open WebUI and Cockpit, remains browser-served and works whether or not a desktop session is open.
- **Kernel 7.0** ships with 26.04 and includes the amdgpu driver for gfx1151.
- **RESOLVED (C21): 24.04.5 evaluated and rejected. Corrected in v0.3.4.** Its point release brings the 7.0 kernel through HWE, but its Mesa stays on 25.2.8 (noble-updates) against 26.0.8 on 26.04 (VERIFIED on packages.ubuntu.com, 2026-10-08), so hardware support is close, not identical. 26.04.1 wins on standard security maintenance to **May 2031** against May 2029 (ubuntu.com/about/release-cycle; the 26.04 release notes say "until April 2031"; Expanded Security Maintenance through Ubuntu Pro runs to May 2036), a newer graphics stack and a newer toolchain. 24.04's one real advantage, officially supported host ROCm, is irrelevant because ROCm runs only inside containers here (Section 3.4). *v0.3.5 (S51): it is no longer an advantage either; AMD's ROCm 10.0.0 support data lists the Ryzen AI Max+ PRO 495 on Ubuntu 26.04 with the GA 7.0 kernel as well as on 24.04.4 (VERIFIED).*
- **RESOLVED (C27, v0.3.4): Fedora 44 KDE Plasma evaluated and rejected; Ubuntu Server re-confirmed by the Principal on 2026-10-08 ("stability is key to a good AI node").** A sourced comparison (seven research strands, each decisive claim checked by two independent verifiers) found: Fedora 44 reaches end of life around June 2027, so the node would need a hands-on major-version upgrade about once a year, four or five times before 2031, each a reboot that TPM unlock, Docker and Remote Desktop must survive unattended; Fedora moved Fedora 44 through four kernel series in five months, while Ubuntu keeps the 7.0 series the Day 1 kernel parameters were tuned for; Fedora's KDE edition is Wayland-only and Plasma 6.8 (due 2026-10-14) removes the X11 session that xrdp needs, leaving only KDE's own remote tool, which joins an already-logged-in session and therefore needs autologin (contrary to Appendix B, R21); AMD's ROCm support list names Ubuntu 26.04.1 for this exact chip and not Fedora. Fedora's real advantages were recorded: a current xrdp (0.10.6.1, with the 2026 security fixes), newer Mesa and firmware, and a Strix Halo community that mostly runs Fedora. The first is mitigated on Ubuntu (R24); the others matter only to the host-built llama.cpp Vulkan backend and can reach Ubuntu through its opt-in HWE kernels if V11 or Phase 3 ever needs them. If the Principal later wants the KDE look, Plasma 6.6 with its X11 session is packaged in 26.04 and could replace XFCE in step 5b with a few lines, behind V19; not adopted.
- **Support coverage (v0.3.4).** Ubuntu's free five-year commitment covers the `main` component. Several packages the node relies on are in `universe`, which gets community fixes only: the desktop and remote layer (xfce4, xrdp, xorgxrdp), Cockpit (cockpit-ws, a login page on the LAN), tpm2-tools and gocryptfs. The DNS fence only looks like one: the `dnsmasq` package (unit and configuration) is in `universe`, but its daemon ships in `dnsmasq-base` in `main` and receives Canonical security updates (2.92-1ubuntu0.4 in resolute-security, packages.ubuntu.com, 2026-10-08). Day 1 keeps Ubuntu Pro off (no telemetry from any installed component, Section 1). For xrdp this matters most, because it has known unpatched flaws reachable before login (R24); Cockpit's login page is in the same support position, has no comparable open list today and is on the watch-list (Section 23); the rest is accepted.
- **RESOLVED (C22): the amd64v3 and amd64v4 archive variants are not adopted.** The benefit falls on CPU-bound distribution packages, not GPU inference, and llama.cpp and the PyTorch containers are already compiled against this exact Zen 5 chip. Canonical kept the standard baseline as 26.04's default after a rollout with known problems; no amd64v4 archive exists. Conversion remains possible post-install if ever wanted.

### 3.2 BIOS — AGREED

| Setting | Value | Why |
|---|---|---|
| UMA frame buffer | Smallest offered, 512 MB if available | On Linux the GPU takes memory dynamically via GTT; a fixed carve-out only wastes it |
| IOMMU | Enabled | Required for containers passing GPU devices |
| fTPM | Enabled | Required for LUKS auto-unlock (Section 3.5). **VERIFY V2** |
| Secure Boot | **Enabled (D15 closed, supersedes D2)** | Ties the TPM2 PCR 7 unlock to this OS image; no out-of-tree kernel modules in this stack, so no signing friction. The Principal enabled it in the BIOS before Phase 1 (confirmed 2026-10-13, Section 22); pre-flight verifies it on the node |

### 3.3 Kernel parameters — AGREED, VERIFY V3

```
amdgpu.gttsize=196608 ttm.pages_limit=50331648 amdgpu.lockup_timeout=10000,60000,10000,10000 panic=10 kho=off
```

**Amended in v0.3.1 (Section 23):** `ttm.pages_limit` is the parameter of record on kernel 7.0; `amdgpu.gttsize` is still honoured but logs a deprecation warning, which V3 treats as expected. `amdgpu.lockup_timeout` is added because DeepSeek V4 Flash triggers a Vulkan DeviceLost on current kernels without it (llama.cpp issue #25664; the 2-second all-queue watchdog default dates from kernel 6.19, so this is not specific to 7.x); without it V22 would fail for a kernel reason, not a model one. The node runs the GA kernel, which stays on the 7.0 series with security and stable backports for the life of the release; the later HWE kernels are opt-in and not used. *v0.3.5 (S45): VERIFIED from the 26.04.1 server ISO itself, which installs `linux-generic`, and from the archive, where the HWE metapackage pulls the very same 7.0 image today (2026-10-09); it is expected to move to the 26.10 kernel (7.3) around the 26.04.2 point release (INFERRED: milestone 2027-02-18, by precedent in -updates up to a month earlier; the exact date is UNKNOWN). Step 4 now checks and holds the GA line anyway, because the 26.04 installer also runs its OEM lookup on Server and a later ISO may default to HWE, and step 5 records it.*

*v0.3.5 (S48; option (c), decided by the Principal on 2026-10-10): the line now also carries `panic=10`, after the three GPU parameters, and no `crashkernel=`. The three GPU parameters still come from `/etc/default/grub.d/90-atlas.cfg`; `panic=10`, and the removal of every `crashkernel=` and every other `panic=`, come from a second drop-in, `/etc/default/grub.d/zzz-atlas-crash.cfg`, which grub-mkconfig sources after every other drop-in on the node, kdump-tools' own included, so it has the last word on the normal and advanced boot entries (a later one that added either parameter would be caught by step 4 and V3a). Recovery entries get no `panic=` (GRUB builds them without `GRUB_CMDLINE_LINUX_DEFAULT`) and keep the kernel's wait-forever default, which suits a boot with someone at the console. Why: Ubuntu's kdump-tools, on the 26.04.1 server image, appends `crashkernel=…,128G-:4096M` to the kernel line through its own drop-in whatever its `USE_KDUMP` setting says, and on this 192 GiB machine kernel 7.0 then sets aside 4096 MiB above 4 GiB plus an automatic 256 MiB below it: 4.25 GiB of MemTotal at every boot (VERIFIED, v7.0 `kernel/crash_reserve.c`). The crash kernel that memory is kept for could not write a useful dump here: its capture initrd has no cryptsetup, so it cannot open the LUKS-encrypted root by TPM or by passphrase (INFERRED; each step VERIFIED). While a crash kernel is loaded, kdump-config sets `kernel.panic_on_oops=1`, turning an oops the kernel would survive into a full crash (VERIFIED). And Ubuntu's kernel waits forever after a panic (`CONFIG_PANIC_TIMEOUT=0`, and no shipped sysctl file changes it; VERIFIED), so a headless node would sit halted until someone cycled the power; with `panic=10` it restarts after 10 seconds and the TPM unlocks the disks unattended, as on any boot (INFERRED). Step 4 switches kdump off without removing the package, and V3a proves the booted line (Section 23, S48).*

*v0.3.5 (S49; decided by the Principal on 2026-10-10): the line ends with `kho=off`, after `panic=10`. It comes from the same `zzz-atlas-crash.cfg`, which also removes every other `kho=` word, so `kho=off` is the only one the kernel sees (with several, the kernel takes the last valid one); recovery entries get none. Why: Ubuntu's 7.0 kernel is built with kexec handover (KHO) switched on by default (`CONFIG_KEXEC_HANDOVER_ENABLE_DEFAULT=y` in 7.0.0-38 and -39; VERIFIED). KHO exists to hand memory state over to a kernel started by kexec, and at every boot it sets aside "scratch" memory for that: twice the kernel's early boot allocations, plus twice node 0's share of them again (VERIFIED, v7.0 `kernel/liveupdate/kexec_handover.c`). On this machine the page map of about 3 GiB dominates those allocations, so the scratch comes to about 13 GiB (12.8 to 13.8; INFERRED). The kernel then hands it to the page allocator as CMA memory, so it stays inside MemTotal; but CMA serves only movable allocations, and the pages behind GTT memory are never movable (VERIFIED, v7.0 `drivers/gpu/drm/ttm/ttm_pool.c`), so with KHO on about 13 GiB of what the Arbiter counts as its pool could never hold GPU memory (4.1). ATLAS never uses kexec handover or the live-update feature built on it. With `kho=off` nothing is set aside, MemTotal stays the same, and all of it can hold GTT pages again. Step 4 checks the generated line and V3a proves the booted one (Section 23, S49).*

*v0.4.0 (S-28; D-36 option (c), approved by the Principal on 2026-10-16 in the returned action register): the kernel line stays exactly as written, and V3b's floor rises with the engines it must hold. No community source has qualified a 192 GB GTT profile, and AMD's footnote caps graphics memory at 160 GB (149.01 GiB; UNVERIFIED on this node), while the two large Day 1 seats are 147 to 151 GiB of files, so a Vulkan heap smaller than the largest seat with its context memory would shut that seat out. V3b therefore requires the largest Day 1 LLM seat on the iGPU with its context memory, as the Arbiter projects it at the profile `engines.json` renders (the exact file bytes, the KV cache and per-slot state at its cache type, and the compute-buffer term), and never less than v0.3.5's 160000 MiB. The two large Day 1 seats project to about 153.6 to 155.1 GiB, GLM-5.3-Flash at 8 × 32k with its mmproj being the larger (INFERRED, 4.1); that is below 160000 MiB (156.25 GiB), so on Day 1 the floor stays 160000 MiB, and a larger seat appended later raises it by itself. Before Phase 2 step 2 has installed the orchestrator the projection is not available, so V3b uses the weights alone and says so; the Phase 2 gate re-runs it with the projection. The line changes only if V3 shows that the 192 GiB request is not honoured; T-01 settles on the node whether the usable heap is the 176 to 189 GiB the request allows or the vendor's cap.*

Sized for 192 GB: `gttsize` is expressed in MiB and `pages_limit` in 4 KiB pages. These were validated on 6.x kernels at 128 GB; **V3** confirms they apply on 7.0 at this capacity, that `rocminfo` reports `gfx1151` as expected, and that `llama-cli --list-devices` sees the large GTT pool (V3b: at least the largest Day 1 LLM seat with its context memory, never below 160000 MiB of Vulkan device memory, v0.4.0 above; the engine budget is the Arbiter's, 4.1). If the reported identifier were ever to differ, the gfx1151 ROCm wheels Phase 4 pins (AMD's since S15, no longer community builds) would not fit it and would need replacing before Phase 4 runs; the Principal has confirmed it reads `gfx1151`.

### 3.4 GPU compute stack — RESOLVED (C2)

Two different stacks serve two different layers, and this is deliberate:

| Layer | Backend | Where it runs | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| LLM inference (llama.cpp) | **Vulkan (RADV)** | Host | Mature on this chip. Matches or beats ROCm for decode. Ships in the box on 26.04, no install. Built from the pinned build tag b11474 (v0.4.0, below) |
| LLM prompt processing, optional | ROCm 10.0.0 + hipBLASLt (v0.4.0, B-13; was ROCm 7.2.2) | A container on Phase 4's pinned ROCm 10.0.0 image, never the host | Faster prefill on long inputs: HIP beats Vulkan on long-prompt prefill by about 10 to 25% in the 2026 research (UNVERIFIED on this node; v0.3.5 said about 3×), and re-prefill now dominates a swap (4.2 rule 4). Phased after Day 1 with D-52 option (b), approved by the Principal on 2026-10-16 in the returned action register: the inbox amdgpu driver only, no DKMS, locked memory for that one container, a pinned image digest and source commit. To-do in Phase 2 step 1's header; it comes in only by a decision after Day 1 to run an LLM engine on ROCm. Not a Day 1 dependency |
| PyTorch multimodal engines | **ROCm inside containers** | Docker/distrobox images carrying their own ROCm userspace | This is how the validated Strix Halo image/video toolboxes work. Host supplies only the kernel driver, `/dev/kfd`, `/dev/dri` |

**llama.cpp pin — v0.4.0 (S-01; D-01 option (c), approved by the Principal on 2026-10-16 in the returned action register).** Phase 2 step 1 builds llama.cpp from build tag `b11474`, commit `b9acf138a1e28ce1fc23b5a4fc4b12444b50f7ea` (2026-10-07, CMake version 0.6.0; VERIFIED by git fetch on 2026-10-16), and refuses to build anything else: the tag must resolve to that commit. It replaces v0.4.1 (commit `b29c606e28a01b1bc8c1351026a0fa6e616bf6c4`, build b10964, 2026-09-14), which cannot load GLM-5.3-Flash: the `glm5-next` architecture and the glm5v vision projector first appear in b11279 (VERIFIED in the git history). `b11474` is a build tag, not a release (v0.6.0 is a different commit), so the build leaves `LLAMA_BUILD_IS_DEV` on and reports 0.6.0-dev. `llama-perplexity` joins the installed binaries, for the Phase 3 quality record and V25. Every source fact the scripts rely on was re-read at `b11474` on 2026-10-16 (VERIFIED in the source unless marked): every flag the units render still exists; the reserved flag and environment lists now carry the real `b11474` names and close gaps that existed at both pins (draft-model, Docker-repository and mmproj-URL pulls, the built-in UI and its MCP proxy, `--tools`, MCP server configurations, prompt logging to disk, the ffmpeg directory, grammar and schema files); the TRACE KV lines still need `--verbosity 4`; a prompt longer than its slot is still rejected with HTTP 400; the `--fit` line the load test parses is unchanged; and context shift is off on every Day 1 chat engine (4.3). The pin changes only by a deliberate edit of Phase 2 step 1; nothing updates llama.cpp unattended.

**The old pin, kept for parity only (S-02, approved by the Principal on 2026-10-16 in the returned action register).** Phase 2 step 1 also builds v0.4.1, refused unless it resolves to its own pinned commit, from a git worktree of the same clone into `/opt/atlas/llama-parity`: `llama-perplexity` alone, never linked into `/usr/local/bin`, never run by a unit, never an engine. Phase 3 uses it once, for the output-parity test V25, a required row of the Phase 3 gate (Section 21): with every LLM seat unloaded through the Arbiter first, the old build writes base logits on a fixed corpus (16 × 512 tokens from the domain profiles) and the new build scores them with `--kl-divergence`, and both builds run the same 24-task deterministic set. The verdict is taken with `-fa on`; `-fa off` is recorded only. Its thresholds were proposed as INFERRED and confirmed by the Principal on 2026-10-17 (S55): a mean KLD of at most 0.01, the same top token at least 98% of the time, and no fewer tasks correct. It covers the chat engines both pins load (gpt-oss-120b, DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 and the router); GLM-5.3-Flash cannot be covered, since v0.4.1 cannot load it, and an old build that fails to load a file records `deferred`.

**Why this resolution matters.** The brief chose 26.04 for the LLM layer, where Vulkan is sufficient, but Phase 4's engines need PyTorch-on-ROCm. Containerised ROCm keeps that stack off the host: the host never needs a ROCm install. *v0.3.5 (S51): the premise first given here, that PyTorch-on-ROCm does not install cleanly on the 26.04 host kernel, is out of date. AMD's ROCm 10.0.0 support data lists the Ryzen AI Max+ PRO 495 on Ubuntu 26.04 with the GA 7.0 kernel and the in-box kernel driver, and 26.04's `universe` carries Ubuntu's own ROCm 7.1 (both VERIFIED, 2026-10-11).* The design stands for its own reasons:

- **Isolation.** The host supplies only the kernel driver, `/dev/kfd` and `/dev/dri`. The ROCm userspace and its Python stack live in an image, not among the host's packages, so they cannot conflict with the host or with the LLM layer's Vulkan stack, and an image can be rebuilt or discarded without touching the host.
- **Exact pinning.** Phase 4 installs ROCm 10.0.0, torch 2.13.0, torchvision 0.28.0 and, since S51, torchaudio 2.11.0.2 from AMD's own wheel index at exact versions, into one image that is built once and never rebuilt by itself (a re-run skips the build).
- **Independence from Ubuntu's ROCm line.** Ubuntu's ROCm is a separate, older line: 7.1 in 26.04's `universe`, 7.2.4 in `-proposed`, 10.0 only in 26.10 (VERIFIED, 2026-10-11). It neither moves the node nor holds it back.

**What changes by itself, and what does not (v0.3.5, S51).** Ubuntu never delivers AMD's ROCm releases to the node. No ROCm or HIP package is installed on the host, and unattended-upgrades upgrades only packages already installed, by default only from the release and security pockets (VERIFIED, unattended-upgrades 2.12ubuntu9), so even a later Canonical update of its own ROCm line would not arrive. ATLAS's ROCm changes only when the Phase 4 pins are edited deliberately and the image is rebuilt. What does change unattended is the kernel: a new GA kernel arrives through `-security` (7.0.0-38 is there today, VERIFIED) and takes effect at the next reboot (R25), and the kernel carries the amdgpu/amdkfd driver that the container's ROCm runs on. So after a kernel ABI change V11 is re-run, by hand: `sudo ./atlas-day1.sh phase4 --force 01` re-runs it on the built image without rebuilding it. No timer or hook does it on its own. A change of kernel line re-tests V11 with the rest (W-KERNEL, 3.7). GPU firmware (`linux-firmware-amd-graphics`) is not in `-security` today (VERIFIED, 2026-10-11), but Ubuntu has published linux-firmware updates with amdgpu changes through `-security` on other releases (noble and questing, March 2026, VERIFIED), so it can change unattended too (INFERRED); V11 is also re-run after a GPU firmware update.

**RISK R1, amended in v0.3.1 and v0.3.5 (S51)** — AMD now publishes gfx1151 PyTorch wheels (ROCm 10.0.0, torch 2.13.0, from AMD's own wheel index), and the Ryzen AI Max+ PRO 495 is on the ROCm support list; the community scottt wheel from 2025 is no longer the path and is not pinned. The residual risk moved to the open hang reports, which v0.3.5 corrects (S51; VERIFIED 2026-10-11 unless marked). They are issues of ROCm/legacy-rocm-build (formerly ROCm/ROCm; new reports go to ROCm/TheRock), not of ROCm/pytorch, where those numbers do not exist. #6182 was closed on 2026-07-15 as a container setup problem (an SELinux policy denying the `/dev/kfd` mapping), not a kernel bug. #6530, the PyTorch hang report, is still open; it reproduces on the 6.17 kernel as well as on 7.0, so it is not specific to kernel 7.0, and the 6.18 kernel v0.3.1 named as the mitigation is not obviously one. Its reporter's workaround is `HSA_ENABLE_INTERRUPT=0`, which also makes the runtime busy-wait; no Day 1 script sets it. The real kernel-7.0 unified-memory deadlock on Strix Halo, #6508, was a kernel bug, fixed in Linux 7.0.13 and in Ubuntu's linux 7.0.0-29.29, so the 26.04.1 ISO kernel (7.0.0-30) and 7.0.0-38 carry the fix. One further report matters only with two or more ROCm processes resident at once: idle clients then pin the GPU at 100% busy on kernels 7.0 and 7.2 (ROCm/TheRock #7578; an AMD triager suspects a known kernel issue, with a fix in progress; the workaround is `GPU_MAX_HW_QUEUES=1` on every ROCm client, which no Day 1 script sets). It was reported with Ollama's bundled ROCm runtime and with ROCm 7.14 PyTorch clients; whether 10.0.0 or 10.1.0 clients trigger it is UNKNOWN. So a V11 failure may come from #6530 rather than the container; that is flagged for the Principal and not acted on during Day 1. Pin the wheel versions: every package the Dockerfile names from AMD's index (rocm, torch, torchvision and, since S51, torchaudio) is pinned exactly. Every Phase 4 engine is tested individually and reported pass/fail. This layer is less battle-tested than the LLM layer and the brief treats it that way.

### 3.5 Disk layout and encryption — GAP filled

**AGREED — Layout.**

| Drive | Mount | Contents |
|---|---|---|
| 4 TB, PCIe 4.0 x4 | `/` (OS), `/var/log`, `/srv/cold`, `/srv/backups` | Ubuntu, XFCE, logs, pruned-memory cold storage, restic repository |
| 8 TB, PCIe 4.0 x4 | `/srv/atlas` | `models/` (GGUF weights), `engines/` (PyTorch weights), `data/` (ChromaDB, graph store, SQLite), `workspace/`, `sandbox/`, `vault/` (encrypted container), `staging/` |

**Why this split, now that both drives are the same speed.** The v0.1 reasoning was bandwidth: keep the fast four-lane drive for weights read under load. That argument is gone; both are four-lane. The remaining argument is capacity and blast radius. The model and engine footprint is the side that grows as capability is added, so it gets the 8 TB drive. The operating system, logs and backups are stable in size and fit the 4 TB comfortably, and keeping the OS on a separate device means a full reinstall never touches the encrypted data volume.

**NEW — Encryption at rest (was unspecified).**

- LUKS2 on the 8 TB data volume and on the OS volume.
- Auto-unlock at boot via `systemd-cryptenroll --tpm2-device=auto`, bound to the firmware TPM, so the headless node boots without a keyboard.
- A recovery passphrase is generated and printed once. **CLOSED (D3):** the Principal keeps one copy on the node for convenience and the authoritative copy on an external USB drive. The USB copy is the one that survives node loss, so it must be stored away from the node, not beside it (R16).
- The vault (Section 11) is a second layer, gocryptfs, on top of the already-encrypted data volume; it protects top-secret files even while the node is running and unlocked.
- Swap is disabled. `/tmp` is tmpfs, capped at 4 GiB since S55 (approved by the Principal on 2026-10-17; it was 16 GiB, the whole host reserve of 4.1): with no swap a tmpfs page cannot be reclaimed, and no Day 1 step writes anywhere near 4 GiB there (INFERRED from an audit of the scripts: model pulls, package caches, builds and large unpacks are on disk). Nothing transient touches disk unencrypted.

### 3.6 Base services — AGREED

- SSH, key-only, password auth off, listening on LAN and WireGuard interfaces only.
- `ufw` default deny inbound; allow SSH, Cockpit, Open WebUI, ntfy on LAN and WireGuard; allow UDP 51820 from anywhere. Default deny outbound except the allowlist (Section 12.5).
- Cockpit for system health and in-browser terminal.
- Time from Canonical's NTS-authenticated servers through chrony, the 26.04 default (v0.3.4): the firewall lets only the chrony daemon's user open UDP 123 and TCP 4460, the same process-level fence as the proxy (80/443) and the DNS forwarder (53).
- Docker with the GPU device nodes passed to containers that need them; the service user in `render` and `video` groups.
- XFCE desktop and xrdp, listening on LAN and WireGuard interfaces only, never internet-facing. **Hardened in v0.3.4 (R24):** only the Xorg session type is offered (the packaged VNC-proxy entry, the path of the most serious 2026 CVE, plus the NeutrinoRDP-proxy and Xvnc entries are switched off), root login and alternate shells are refused, TLS only; `RDP_ALLOW_FROM` (addresses inside the LAN) optionally narrows the LAN side to the Principal's PC. Google Chrome installed in the desktop session for the Principal's own use (the Principal's choice, v0.3.3; Google's apt repository, managed policy with metrics, sign-in, sync and background mode off).

**Hardware watchdog — v0.3.5 (S50; decided by the Principal on 2026-10-11).** systemd (PID 1) arms the platform's watchdog timer at every boot and pings it. If systemd or the whole kernel stops, the pings stop, the timer runs out and the hardware resets the node; the TPM then unlocks the disks unattended, as after `panic=10` (INFERRED, as in S48). The settings are in `/etc/systemd/system.conf.d/90-atlas-watchdog.conf`: `RuntimeWatchdogSec=2min` (an idle systemd pings every minute, a busy one at most every 30 seconds, and the reset comes two minutes after the last good ping) and `RebootWatchdogSec=10min` (systemd's own default, written out; it bounds the last phase of a reboot). Two minutes rather than 30 seconds: on other Strix Halo machines a 30-second timeout reset live machines that would have recovered (a hung network mount, memory-reclaim thrash), and this node has no swap and loads up to about 160 GiB into memory; systemd raised its own services' watchdogs to three minutes after disk stalls of over a minute (the value is a judgement, INFERRED; the timing it rests on is VERIFIED in systemd 259.5). The timer is the TCO timer of the chipset's SMBus controller (PCI 1022:790b, revision 0x71 on Strix Halo, VERIFIED; on this Gorgon Halo board INFERRED until Phase 1 step 4 reads it), driven by the kernel's `sp5100_tco` driver (VERIFIED, kernel 7.0). Ubuntu blacklists every watchdog module, and udev, `/etc/modules-load.d` and `modprobe@.service` all honour that blacklist, so a small unit, `atlas-watchdog-driver.service`, loads the driver by name before `sysinit.target` at every boot (VERIFIED, systemd 259.5 and kmod 34.2). If step 4 finds no hardware watchdog (the driver says "Watchdog hardware is disabled" when the firmware has switched the timer off; no Strix Halo log read shows that, and this board is UNKNOWN until step 4), the ACPI WDAT watchdog is used when the firmware describes one (none seen on Strix Halo), and otherwise the kernel's software watchdog, `softdog`, stands in, recorded as deferred with the to-do `watchdog`: it still resets a frozen or crashed systemd, but not reliably a hard lockup of the whole kernel, since its timer needs a working timer interrupt (INFERRED); with `soft_panic=1` its expiry is a real panic, so a pstore record and then the `panic=10` restart. Only systemd may hold the watchdog device: opening it starts the timer, so no `wdctl`, no `cat /dev/watchdog*` and no watchdog daemon. V24 proves the timer is armed (Section 21); whether this board's reset completes stays unproven until the guided test reset on the watch-list (W-DRILL, 3.7), which the live ATLAS leads with the Principal present; no Day 1 script runs it. What the watchdog covers, set against what `panic=10` alone left uncovered (S48):

| What stops the node | With `panic=10` alone (S48) | With the watchdog (S50) |
|---|---|---|
| A hard hang of the whole kernel that never panics | Hangs until the power is cycled | Reset at most 2 minutes after systemd's last ping: the hardware timer counts on its own (INFERRED; field logs from a GMKtec EVO-X2, Strix Halo, show such resets). Not reliably with the softdog fallback |
| systemd hangs: a busy loop, a deadlock, a wait on a hung disk | Hangs | Reset at most 2 minutes after its last ping (VERIFIED, systemd 259.5) |
| systemd crashes | Freezes (`CrashAction=freeze`, the default) | Reset 2 minutes later: the freeze closes the watchdog device without the magic 'V' that would stop it, so the timer keeps running and nothing pings it (VERIFIED, systemd 259.5 and kernel 7.0) |
| systemd's last reboot phase hangs | Hangs | Reset after 10 minutes |
| A kernel panic once the kernel has read its parameters | Restarts after 10 seconds | Unchanged: `panic=10` restarts first and the pstore record is kept; if that restart itself hangs, the timer still fires, because the kernel's emergency restart does not stop it (VERIFIED) |
| A panic before the kernel reads its parameters, or a hang before the driver loads | Hangs | Hangs: not covered |
| A soft lockup, a hard lockup of some CPUs, a hung task or a GPU wedge while systemd still runs | Warns only | Warns only: systemd keeps pinging. No lockup panic was added (Ubuntu's defaults leave them off; each would be a separate decision) |
| A failed TPM unseal waiting at the LUKS passphrase prompt; dracut's emergency shell | Waits | Waits, and no timer started in this boot runs there: the watchdog modules are in the initramfs but Ubuntu's blacklist, copied in, keeps them unloaded (INFERRED, dracut 110). But a timer left running when the board reset (a `panic=10` or sysrq emergency restart does not stop it, VERIFIED) is stopped only when the driver loads in the real root, and whether this board's reset clears it first is UNKNOWN; if it does not, a wait in that boot can be reset once (W-DRILL checks it) |
| `emergency.target`, `rescue.target`, the recovery boot entry | Wait | Wait: systemd stays alive and keeps pinging (after a failure that leads to `emergency.target` the driver is already loaded and armed; a boot straight into `emergency.target` or `rescue.target` never loads it). The same caveat as the row above applies after a reset |
| A hang in the kernel's own device shutdown at reboot (amdgpu reboot deadlocks are reported on Strix Halo) | Hangs | Hangs: the kernel stops the timer just before that phase (VERIFIED) |
| Poweroff or halt | — | systemd disarms the timer first, so a hung poweroff never becomes a reset (VERIFIED) |
| A suspend | — | UNKNOWN on this chipset: neither the driver nor systemd does anything for sleep. Nothing on the node suspends by itself, and the sleep targets are not masked |

Accepted, as with `panic=10`: a hang that recurs at every boot once the driver has loaded restarts the node in a loop, every two minutes plus the boot, plus GRUB's 30-second menu when the reset comes before the boot completes; the PIN-less TPM key is exempt from dictionary-attack lockout, so the loop cannot lock the TPM (S48). A `systemctl daemon-reexec`, which every systemd upgrade runs, disarms and re-arms the timer, leaving a moment unguarded. Recovery after a reset takes about 3 to 4.5 minutes in all (INFERRED; this board's POST time is UNKNOWN). Added by default, the Principal's call: the softdog fallback where the board has no usable hardware watchdog. The test reset, with the GRUB hold that shows whether the timer survives a reset, is on the watch-list for the live ATLAS to lead with the Principal present (W-DRILL, 3.7). Left to the Principal, nothing added: the timeout value, lockup panics, `CrashAction=reboot`, `watchdog.stop_on_reboot=0`, masking the sleep targets, and loading the driver in the initramfs (Section 23, S50).

### 3.7 Platform watch-list — considered, not adopted (v0.3.5; additions in v0.4.0)

Platform changes reviewed and deliberately left out, each with the event that would bring it in. Raised by the report "Linux 7.4 to support the NPU on AMD Ryzen AI Max 400 series Gorgon Halo" (Phoronix, 30 September 2026), reviewed 2026-10-09: a standard device-name update with nothing to adopt. Nothing here is adopted. Day 1 is touched in two ways: step 4 holds the GA 7.0 kernel line that W-KERNEL relies on (S45: HWE metapackages over the same 7.0 image are swapped for `linux-generic` after a clean apt simulation unless a GA or HWE kernel metapackage is held with `apt-mark`; anything else, a held one included, becomes the to-do `kernel-line`), and step 5 adds two read-only records (the kernel line, which closes that to-do or raises it again with the state after the reboot, and what the NPU shows). W-DRILL, added at the Principal's request on 2026-10-11, is of another kind: not a change left out but a set of tests deferred until the live ATLAS can lead them with the Principal present. The engine watch-list is 15.5; the build's other watch items are listed at the end of Section 23. *v0.4.0:* five items from the Lucebox review of 2026-10-15 join the list, approved by the Principal on 2026-10-16 in the returned action register (D-62 (a), D-63 (a), D-64 (a)): W-DGPU, W-PLACEMENT, W-MEMGUARD, W-MANIFEST and W-SESSIONLOG. Like the rest, none is adopted and none changes Day 1.

| Item | What it is | Why not now | What would change the answer |
|---|---|---|---|
| W-NPU: the XDNA 2 NPU (reviewed 2026-10-09) | The NPU inside the PRO 495. The Linux 7.4 patch ("accel/amdxdna: Add support for AIE2 revision 9", posted 2026-09-29) adds two lines that give it the name "NPU Gorgon Halo"; the driver binds by PCI identity (1022:17f0 at PCI revision 0x10, 0x11 or 0x20), not by this name table, so the patch changes no binding: a matching NPU binds under a generic name without it. That this chip matches is INFERRED (one user report of 1022:17f0 on a 7.2.8 kernel; no primary source for its revision), which is why step 5 records it. Kernel 7.0 has no name table at all, so the patch cannot apply to it. The in-tree `amdxdna` driver and the NPU firmware already ship in 26.04 (`linux-modules-7.0.0-*-generic`, `linux-firmware-amd-misc`). The only Linux NPU runtime is FastFlowLM (AMD/ROCm since v1.0.0, 2026-08-11; v1.0.7 has an Ubuntu 26.04 package whose XRT libraries are in `universe`); Lemonade reaches the NPU only through it | Nothing ATLAS runs gains from it. No NPU build of the retrieval pair (bge-m3 and bge-reranker-v2-m3 at the 2026-10-09 review; Qwen3-Embedding-4B and Qwen3-Reranker-4B, on the CPU, since v0.4.0); the router model exists only in FastFlowLM's own 4-bit format and community figures put the NPU several times slower than the iGPU; the NPU shares the same 192 GB and the same bandwidth, so moving work to it frees nothing. FastFlowLM does not list this chip, runs closed-source NPU kernels under licence terms that contradict each other (README "free for any use", TERMS.md a USD 10M revenue threshold), loads one model per process and downloads models from Hugging Face, and the community container known to work needs `seccomp=unconfined`, unlimited memlock and a world-writable `/dev/accel/accel0`, where ATLAS's service containers drop their capabilities and keep the default seccomp profile (the one exception is Phase 4: if V11 shows on this node that the GPU self-test fails under the default profile and passes without it, Phase 4's GPU test containers run with `seccomp=unconfined`, recorded and warned). Getting Linux 7.4 code would mean leaving the GA kernel (3.3, S45) for a name | FastFlowLM (or Lemonade) lists Gorgon Halo / Ryzen AI Max 400 and Ubuntu 26.04 as supported; its licence is settled in writing; an NPU build of a model ATLAS uses appears; a like-for-like speech-to-text measurement on Strix Halo or Gorgon Halo beats the CPU path on latency or energy without accuracy loss (Whisper is the only plausible candidate); a runtime that works under ATLAS's container hardening with models installed offline. Separately: a Gorgon Halo NPU fix in the 26.04 GA kernel by SRU (Launchpad bug 2169534, title seen only), or `amdxdna` errors in the step-5 record, which the normal SRU flow handles (blacklisting `amdxdna` is the fallback if it ever misbehaves) |
| W-PERFOPT: AMD IOMMU "PerfOpt" for integrated GPUs (reviewed 2026-10-09) | A Linux 7.4 change that lets the iGPU bypass IOMMU translation when it is in identity mode (`amdgpu.iommu_perfopt=0` turns it off) | Not in the 7.0 kernel. Reported gains disagree (18 to 23% in one report, a typical 2 to 4% on Strix Halo in another), and it switches off the IOMMU's containment of GPU memory access (ATS, PRI, PASID and SVA disabled) on a node that enables the IOMMU (3.2); its merge was not confirmed from a primary source | It reaches the 26.04 GA kernel by SRU, or independent llama.cpp Vulkan benchmarks on Strix or Gorgon Halo show at least 10% with the DMA trade-off reviewed; or ATLAS deliberately changes kernel line (W-KERNEL) |
| W-KERNEL: newer kernel lines (reviewed 2026-10-09) | The 26.04 HWE kernels: the 26.10 kernel (7.3) is expected with the 26.04.2 point release (INFERRED; milestone 2027-02-18, possibly in `-updates` a month earlier); 26.04.3 the 27.04 kernel, the first stock Ubuntu kernel built on 7.4 code, around August 2027 by precedent; the line settles on the 28.04 kernel at 26.04.5 (about August or September 2028). The OEM kernels (`linux-oem-26.04`, `-26.04a`, `-26.04b`) are still 7.0-based with a shorter life | The GA line stays on 7.0, the kernel the Section 3.3 parameters were validated on, with standard support to May 2031. The HWE line moves to a new kernel series about every six months until 2028, each a re-validation of V3a, V11 and V22 on an unattended node. Day 1 holds the node on GA (S45) | A V-check or a driver feature that only a newer kernel provides, decided by the Principal as a deliberate change of line; then V3a, V11, V22, W-NPU and W-PERFOPT are re-tested together |
| W-DRILL: guided crash and watchdog tests, led by the live ATLAS with the Principal present (added 2026-10-11 at the Principal's request) | Day 1 arms the crash restart (`panic=10`, S48) and the hardware watchdog (S50) but never proves that they work on this board. In a session the Principal has agreed to, ATLAS explains each test and its risk in plain words, waits for the Principal's go-ahead before each one, runs it, and records the result. (1) Crash restart: with all work written to disk (`sync`), a test panic (`echo c > /proc/sysrq-trigger`). This proves that `panic=10` restarts the node, that the TPM then unlocks both disks unattended, and whether a panic record reaches `/var/lib/systemd/pstore` (step 5 recorded the pstore backend). (2) Watchdog reset: ATLAS stops systemd's pings and lets the timer expire, about two minutes. This proves that this board's FCH timer really resets the node, again with the unattended unlock, and reads the reset cause on the next boot (the device's `bootstatus`, the kernel log). (3) Timer survival: after one of those resets, the Principal holds the next boot at the GRUB menu for over two minutes. A second reset means the timer outlives the board's reset, and loading the driver in the initramfs (S50) becomes a decision for the Principal. (4) Suspend: either a short suspend with the timer armed, to see whether it resets the node, or the Principal's choice to mask the sleep targets instead. Each UNKNOWN in S48 and S50 that a test settles is written back to this document as VERIFIED, with the date. Phase 1 step 5 puts the tests on the to-do list the live ATLAS works from (`w-drill`), and raises them again whenever step 5 runs after the live ATLAS has closed them | Day 1 never triggers a panic or a reset: its phases run largely unattended, and a test that goes wrong (a failed unlock, a restart loop) needs the Principal at the console with the LUKS recovery keys (D3). The tests also need the live ATLAS and its records, which exist only once Day 1 is complete. A deliberate panic or reset is never ATLAS's own decision: it runs only in a session the Principal has agreed to and attends | Day 1 complete (the Phase 4 gate passed) and the live ATLAS running: ATLAS then proposes the first session, for an idle node (no engine loaded, no task running, the night's AEGIS backup done), with the Principal at the console and the recovery keys at hand. ATLAS proposes the tests again after a BIOS or firmware update, a change of kernel line (W-KERNEL), a TPM re-seal, or any change to the S48 or S50 settings, since each can change the restart or unlock path |
| W-DGPU: a discrete GPU (added 2026-10-16; D-62 option (a), and D-63 option (a) for a second machine) | A Radeon AI PRO R9700 (32 GB) in a USB4 v2 dock with a supply of 750 W or more, as a second device with its own memory pool. Its kernel parameters (`thunderbolt.host_reset=false`, `amdgpu.runpm=0`) and its power and noise limits would then join Sections 2, 3.3, 4.1 and 4.2 (S-55). A second machine, the Lucebox Zero 495 appliance, is not adopted either: it duplicates this node's processor, and its software checks for updates daily | Lucebox's own engine measured large gains with the card for DeepSeek V4.1 and Qwen3.8-Flash-Next but almost none for the 0731 checkpoint ATLAS runs, and those are not the node's mainline Vulkan figures (UNVERIFIED here). The card costs about USD 1,800 before an unpriced dock and supply, never idles down with `amdgpu.runpm=0`, and brings a 300 W blower near 3,000 RPM | The DeepSeek V4.1 trial (T-30, phased after Day 1) shows V4.1 or Qwen3.8-Flash-Next worth accelerating, preferably once a 32 GB card in a USB4 dock has been published working; the Principal then supplies a budget ceiling, the tolerance for the blower's noise and a second power brick, and whether external hardware is acceptable |
| W-PLACEMENT: usage-ranked expert placement (added 2026-10-16; D-64 (a), B-62) | The most-called experts of a MoE engine sit in the fastest memory, ranked by a routing profile measured on the node. llama.cpp's `-ot` tensor overrides are its manual form | The node has one memory pool, so there is nothing to rank between | W-DGPU is adopted: this is then the design to follow for the card's 32 GB |
| W-MEMGUARD: Lucebox Manage's memory guard (added 2026-10-16; D-64 (a), B-63) | A guard that stops a program filling memory before the machine can freeze | Partly covered already: the Arbiter's hard pre-flight against a budget that keeps a fixed 16 GiB host reserve (4.1, 4.3) is the same idea, and a second guard would duplicate it | The next baseline revision compares it with the Arbiter, rather than adding a second guard now |
| W-MANIFEST: signed release manifests that expire (added 2026-10-16; D-64 (a), B-64) | Lucebox signs its releases with Ed25519, and a manifest expires after 120 days | Phase 4 and the llama.cpp builds pin exact versions and build once, with no expiry; the idea implies no updater and no outbound check, but nothing on Day 1 needs it | A decision to re-attest the node's own pinned images on a schedule, as a cheap discipline with no updater and no outbound check |
| W-SESSIONLOG: retrieval from local agent session logs (added 2026-10-16; D-64 (a), B-65) | Lucebox's "Learning" without the marketing: relevant past work retrieved from local agent session logs into the prompt; retrieval, not training (its LoRA adapters are "Coming soon") | The Vector Cortex and the graph layer (10.1, 10.2) index memory and documents, not session logs, and nothing on Day 1 needs them | The live ATLAS has session logs worth retrieving: they then become a candidate source for the existing retrieval stack, not a new one |
